The defined term in Hindi must be the exact Hindi equivalent used consistently throughout the instrument. Cross-references: "regulation 5(2)(a)" → "विनियम 5(2)(क)". The parentheses and clause letters follow Devanagari conventions.

Penalty and adjudication clauses use precise terminology: "penalty" → "शास्ति" (not "जुर्माना" which is generic fine; "शास्ति" is the statutory term in SEBI Act, RBI Act, Competition Act), "adjudicating officer" → "निर्णय अधिकारी", "show cause notice" → "कारण बताओ सूचना", "personal hearing" → "व्यक्तिगत सुनवाई", "order imposing penalty" → "शास्ति अधिरोपित करने वाला आदेश". "Compounding" (of offences) → "समाधान" (not "समझौता"). "Compounding application" → "समाधान आवेदन". "Compounding fee" → "समाधान शुल्क". The translator must verify the parent Act's Hindi text for the exact term; for instance, the Companies Act, 2013 uses "समाधान" for compounding; the Customs Act uses "समाधान" as well. Consistency across the regulatory corpus is mandatory.

Transitional and savings provisions: "Notwithstanding anything contained in these regulations..." → "इन विनियमों में अंतर्विष्ट किसी बात के होते हुए भी...". "Savings" → "बचत उपबंध" (for savings clause). "Repeal and savings" → "निरसन और बचत". "Validation" → "विधिमान्यकरण". "Grandfathering" (protection of existing rights) → "पूर्ववर्ती अधिकारों का संरक्षण" or "ग्रैंडफादरिंग" (transliterated, increasingly used in SEBI/RBI notifications). The translator must follow the specific regulator's established usage.

  • Using "जुर्माना" for statutory "शास्ति" in SEBI, RBI, Competition Act notifications — marks the translation as non-legal.
  • Translating "bye-law" as "उपनियम" in a context where the parent Act uses "विनियम" for the same instrument — breaks the statutory definition chain.
  • Rendering "hereby" as "इसके द्वारा" instead of "एतद्वारा" in Gazette notifications — register breach in delegated legislation.
  • Converting SI unit symbols to Hindi words (e.g., "kg" → "किलोग्राम" in a table cell) — violates Legal Metrology Act and BIS standards.
  • Using "समझौता" for "compounding of offences" instead of "समाधान" — changes the legal nature of the proceeding.
  • Translating "Returning Officer" as "निर्वाचन अधिकारी" instead of "रिटर्निंग अधिकारी" per ECI glossary — terminology violation in electoral documents.
  • Rendering "infructuous expenditure" as "व्यर्थ व्यय" instead of "निष्फल व्यय" in CAG audit reports — loses the audit classification precision.

Translation of Budget Documents and Finance Bill Explanatory Memoranda

Budget speeches, the Finance Bill, and the accompanying Explanatory Memoranda constitute a hybrid register blending parliamentary rhetoric, statutory drafting, and fiscal accounting. The translator must preserve the performative force of the Finance Minister’s opening address — “I propose to…”, “The Government has decided to…”, “It is proposed to amend…” — while rendering the precise legislative language of the Bill’s clauses. In Hindi, the conventional opening “मैं प्रस्ताव करता हूँ…” (masculine) or “मैं प्रस्ताव करती हूँ…” (feminine) follows the speaker’s gender; the official Hindi version of the Union Budget typically uses the masculine form irrespective of the Minister’s gender, treating the office (“वित्त मंत्री”) as masculine. The Explanatory Memorandum follows a rigid template: “Clause X seeks to amend Section Y of the Z Act, 20XX…” → “खंड X, Z अधिनियम, 20XX की धारा Y में संशोधन करना चाहता है…”. The verb “seeks to” is conventionally rendered as “चाहता है” (not “प्रयास करता है”) to retain legislative intent without diluting certainty.

Fiscal terminology demands exact equivalents: “Revenue receipts” → “राजस्व प्राप्तियाँ”, “Capital receipts” → “पूंजीगत प्राप्तियाँ”, “Fiscal deficit” → “राजकोषीय घाटा”, “Primary deficit” → “प्राथमिक घाटा”, “Revenue deficit” → “राजस्व घाटा”. “Tax buoyancy” is translated as “कर लोचशीलता” and “tax elasticity” as “कर लोच”. “Cess” remains “उपकर”; “surcharge” → “अधिभार”. “Budget estimate” (BE) and “revised estimate” (RE) are rendered as “बजट अनुमान” and “संशोधित अनुमान” respectively, with the abbreviations BE/RE retained in parentheses in the Hindi text. “Fiscal consolidation roadmap” becomes “राजकोषीय सुदृढ़ीकरण रोडमैप” (roadmap transliterated) as no settled Hindi compound exists in the official glossary.

Key Budget Term Mapping
English TermHindi EquivalentRegister Note
Finance Billवित्त विधेयकStatutory title, capitalised
Explanatory Memorandumव्याख्यात्मक ज्ञापनFormal, used in Gazette
Appropriation Billविनियोग विधेयकDistinct from Finance Bill
Vote on AccountलेखानुदानConstitutional term (Art. 116)
Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management Actराजकोषीय उत्तरदायित्व और बजट प्रबंधन अधिनियमFull official title

The translator must also respect numbering conventions: clauses of the Finance Bill are numbered in Arabic numerals (1, 2, 3…) while the Explanatory Memorandum refers to them as “Clause 1”, “Clause 2”. In Hindi, “Clause” → “खंड” and the numeral follows Devanagari digits (१, २, ३…) in the official Hindi Gazette; the English version retains Arabic numerals. Consistency across the bilingual pair is verified against the Gazette publication.

Translation of Public Procurement Tender Documents and Bid Evaluation Reports

Tender notices, Request for Proposal (RFP) documents, and bid evaluation reports operate in a highly formulaic administrative register. The opening formula “The President of India / Governor of [State] invites sealed tenders…” translates to “भारत के राष्ट्रपति / [राज्य] के राज्यपाल सीलबंद निविदाएँ आमंत्रित करते हैं…”. “Sealed tender” → “सीलबंद निविदा”; “open tender” → “खुली निविदा”; “limited tender” → “सीमित निविदा”. “Earnest Money Deposit (EMD)” becomes “अग्रिम जमा राशि (EMD)” with the acronym retained. “Performance Security” → “निष्पादन प्रतिभूति”; “Bank Guarantee” → “बैंक गारंटी”. “Technical Bid” and “Financial Bid” are rendered as “तकनीकी बोली” and “वित्तीय बोली” respectively; “bid” is consistently “बोली” throughout to avoid synonym drift.

Evaluation criteria tables contain weighted scores: “Technical Score (70%)” → “तकनीकी अंक (७०%)”, “Financial Score (30%)” → “वित्तीय अंक (३०%)”. “Lowest Evaluated Bid (LEB)” → “न्यूनतम मूल्यांकित बोली (LEB)”. “Letter of Award (LoA)” → “अधिकरण पत्र (LoA)”. “Contract Agreement” → “अनुबंध समझौता”. “Force Majeure” remains in Latin script in the Hindi version, with a gloss “अप्रत्याशित घटना” in parentheses at first occurrence. “Liquidated Damages” → “निर्धारित क्षतिपूर्ति”; “Penalty for Delay” → “विलंब के लिए शास्ति”. The distinction between “penalty” (शास्ति) and “liquidated damages” (निर्धारित क्षतिपूर्ति) mirrors statutory usage in the Indian Contract Act, 1872 (Section 74).

Bid evaluation reports follow a narrative structure: “The Evaluation Committee examined the technical bids…”, “The financial bids were opened on…”, “The Committee recommends award of contract to M/s XYZ…”. In Hindi, “Evaluation Committee” → “मूल्यांकन समिति”; “recommends” → “सिफ़ारिश करती है” (feminine singular because “समिति” is feminine). “Subject to” in conditions precedent is rendered as “अधीन” or “के अधीन” (e.g., “subject to verification of documents” → “दस्तावेज़ों के सत्यापन के अधीन”). The translator must preserve the passive voice where the English source uses it (“The contract shall be signed…”) because the Hindi official style prefers the impersonal “हस्ताक्षर किया जाएगा” over an active agent.

Worked Translation Exercise
Translate the following tender clause into Hindi, preserving register and terminology: “The Bidder shall furnish a Performance Security of 5% of the Contract Value in the form of a Bank Guarantee valid for 180 days beyond the date of completion of the Contract.”
निविदादाता को अनुबंध मूल्य का ५% निष्पादन प्रतिभूति, बैंक गारंटी के रूप में, जो अनुबंध की पूर्णता की तारीख से १८० दिन आगे तक वैध हो, प्रस्तुत करनी होगी।
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a tender clause contains a percentage, a monetary amount, and a validity period, translate numeric values into Devanagari digits (५%, १८०) as used in the official Hindi Gazette; retain acronyms (EMD, LoA, LEB) in Latin script with the Hindi expansion at first occurrence. This mirrors the bilingual Gazette layout and avoids deduction for incorrect numeral script.

Translation of Environmental Impact Assessment Summaries and Clearance Conditions

Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) reports and the ensuing Environmental Clearance (EC) orders combine scientific description, statutory conditioning, and quasi‑judicial reasoning. The executive summary typically opens with “The project proponent proposes to establish…”, rendered as “परियोजना प्रस्तावक … स्थापित करने का प्रस्ताव करता है”. “Project proponent” is consistently “परियोजना प्रस्तावक” (not “प्रवर्तक”). “Terms of Reference (ToR)” → “संदर्भ की शर्तें (ToR)”. “Baseline data” → “आधारभूत आंकड़े”. “Impact prediction” → “प्रभाव पूर्वानुमान”. “Mitigation measures” → “शमन उपाय”. “Environmental Management Plan (EMP)” → “पर्यावरण प्रबंधन योजना (EMP)”. The clearance order uses the formula “In exercise of the powers conferred by Section 3 of the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986… the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change hereby grants Environmental Clearance…” → “पर्यावरण (संरक्षण) अधिनियम, 1986 की धारा ३ द्वारा प्रदत्त शक्तियों का प्रयोग करते हुए, पर्यावरण, वन और जलवायु परिवर्तन मंत्रालय एतद्वारा पर्यावरणीय मंजूरी प्रदान करता है…”. The verb “grants” agrees with the masculine singular “मंत्रालय”.

Conditions attached to the EC are numbered and each begins with “The project proponent shall…”. In Hindi, “shall” is rendered as “करेगा/करेगी” according to the subject’s gender; since “प्रस्तावक” is masculine, “करेगा” is used. Typical conditions: “The project proponent shall install Continuous Emission Monitoring System (CEMS)…” → “परियोजना प्रस्तावक सतत उत्सर्जन निगरानी प्रणाली (CEMS) स्थापित करेगा…”. “The project proponent shall submit half‑yearly compliance reports…” → “परियोजना प्रस्तावक अर्धवार्षिक अनुपालन रिपोर्ट प्रस्तुत करेगा…”. “Half‑yearly” → “अर्धवार्षिक”; “quarterly” → “त्रैमासिक”. “Compliance report” → “अनुपालन रिपोर्ट”. “Non‑compliance” → “अननुपालन”. “Revocation of clearance” → “मंजूरी का निरसन”. “Public hearing” → “जन सुनवाई”. “Stakeholder consultation” → “हितधारक परामर्श”. The translator must retain acronyms CEMS, EMP, ToR, EC in Latin script, as the Hindi Gazette does, and provide the Hindi expansion at first occurrence.

EIA/EC Term Mapping
EnglishHindiNote
Environmental Clearanceपर्यावरणीय मंजूरीStatutory term
Project Proponentपरियोजना प्रस्तावकConsistent across documents
Mitigation Measuresशमन उपायPlural noun phrase
Continuous Emission Monitoring Systemसतत उत्सर्जन निगरानी प्रणाली (CEMS)Acronym retained
Half‑yearly Compliance Reportअर्धवार्षिक अनुपालन रिपोर्टFixed collocation

The clearance order often ends with a “Validity” clause: “This clearance shall remain valid for a period of 10 years from the date of issue…” → “यह मंजूरी जारी होने की तारीख से १० वर्ष की अवधि के लिए वैध रहेगी…”. The feminine “रहेगी” agrees with “मंजूरी”. The translator must ensure the numeral “10” appears as “१०” in the Hindi version.

Translation of Labour Court Awards and Industrial Tribunal Orders

Labour Court awards under the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, and Industrial Tribunal orders employ a quasi‑judicial register blending statutory interpretation with factual findings. The heading “Award” translates as “पंचाट” (not “निर्णय”, used for civil judgments). “Industrial Tribunal” → “औद्योगिक अधिकरण”. “Reference” (the government’s referral of a dispute) → “संदर्भ”. “Claimant” → “दावेदार”; “Respondent” → “प्रतिवादी”. “The dispute relates to…” → “विवाद … से संबंधित है”. “The Tribunal finds that…” → “अधिकरण यह पाता है कि…”. “The Tribunal holds that…” → “अधिकरण यह अभिनिर्धारित करता है कि…”. “Holds” is rendered as “अभिनिर्धारित करता है” to reflect the authoritative declaration.

Remedies use precise terminology: “Reinstatement with full back wages” → “पूर्ण पिछले वेतन सहित पुनर्नियुक्ति”. “Compensation in lieu of reinstatement” → “पुनर्नियुक्ति के बदले क्षतिपूर्ति”. “Continuity of service” → “सेवा की निरंतरता”. “Seniority” → “वरिष्ठता”. “Gratuity” → “उपदान”. “Provident Fund” → “भविष्य निधि”. “Bonus” → “बोनस”. “The employer shall pay…” → “नियोक्ता … भुगतान करेगा”. “The workman shall be entitled to…” → “कर्मकार … का हकदार होगा”. “Workman” is consistently “कर्मकार” (not “श्रमिक”, which is generic). “Industrial dispute” → “औद्योगिक विवाद”. “Lay‑off” → “अस्थायी निलंबन”; “Retrenchment” → “सेवा समाप्ति” — the Act distinguishes these; the translator must follow the Hindi text of the Act.

Orders often contain a “Costs” clause: “Each party shall bear its own costs” → “प्रत्येक पक्ष अपना खर्च स्वयं वहन करेगा”. “Costs” → “खर्च”. “The award shall be published in the Official Gazette” → “पंचाट राजपत्र में प्रकाशित किया जाएगा”. The passive “shall be published” → “प्रकाशित किया जाएगा”. The translator must preserve the formal “shall” distinction: active obligations on parties use “करेगा”; procedural steps on the award itself use passive “किया जाएगा”.

Translation of Tax Administration Circulars and Advance Ruling Orders

Tax administration circulars issued by the Central Board of Direct Taxes (CBDT) or the Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs (CBIC) and Advance Ruling orders under the Income‑tax Act, 1961, and the Customs Act, 1962, demand terminological fidelity to statutory definitions. “Circular” → “परिपत्र”. “Instruction” → “अनुदेश”. “Clarification” → “स्पष्टीकरण”. “Advance Ruling” → “अग्रिम विनिर्णय”. “Applicant” → “आवेदक”. “Authority for Advance Ruling (AAR)” → “अग्रिम विनिर्णय प्राधिकरण (AAR)”. “Question of law” → “विधि का प्रश्न”. “Question of fact” → “तथ्य का प्रश्न”. “Binding on the applicant and the department” → “आवेदक और विभाग पर बाध्यकारी”. “The ruling shall be binding…” → “यह विनिर्णय … बाध्यकारी होगा”.

Circulars often begin with “In exercise of the powers conferred by Section 119 of the Income‑tax Act, 1961, the Central Board of Direct Taxes hereby issues the following instructions…” → “आयकर अधिनियम, 1961 की धारा ११९ द्वारा प्रदत्त शक्तियों का प्रयोग करते हुए, केंद्रीय प्रत्यक्ष कर बोर्ड एतद्वारा निम्नलिखित अनुदेश जारी करता है…”. The verb “issues” agrees with the masculine singular “बोर्ड”. “Hereby” → “एतद्वारा”. The body uses “It is clarified that…” → “यह स्पष्ट किया जाता है कि…”. “It is directed that…” → “यह निर्देशित किया जाता है कि…”. “It is requested that…” → “यह अनुरोध किया जाता है कि…”. The passive impersonal construction is rendered with “किया जाता है” to preserve the bureaucratic tone.

Advance Ruling orders follow a structured format: “Facts”, “Questions”, “Analysis”, “Ruling”. “Facts” → “तथ्य”. “Questions” → “प्रश्न”. “Analysis” → “विश्लेषण”. “Ruling” → “विनिर्णय”. The ruling paragraph ends with “Accordingly, the Authority rules that…” → “तदनुसार, प्राधिकरण यह विनिर्णय देता है कि…”. “Subject to the provisions of…” → “… के प्रावधानों के अधीन”. “Notwithstanding anything contained in…” → “… में अंतर्विष्ट किसी बात के होते हुए भी” (or the statutory formula “किसी बात के होते हुए भी”). The translator must replicate the exact statutory phrasing used in the Hindi version of the Income‑tax Act to avoid register breach.

Tax Circular and Advance Ruling Term Mapping
EnglishHindiRegister Note
Central Board of Direct Taxesकेंद्रीय प्रत्यक्ष कर बोर्डOfficial name
Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customsकेंद्रीय अप्रत्यक्ष कर और सीमा शुल्क बोर्डOfficial name
Advance Rulingअग्रिम विनिर्णयStatutory term
Binding on the applicantआवेदक पर बाध्यकारीFixed collocation
Notwithstanding anything contained inकिसी बात के होते हुए भीStatutory formula

Numerical references to sections, rules, and notifications must use Devanagari digits in the Hindi text (e.g., “Section 119” → “धारा ११९”). The abbreviations “CBDT” and “CBIC” are retained in Latin script; their Hindi expansions are given at first occurrence. The phrase “dated” in citations (“Circular No. 5/2023 dated 12‑03‑2023”) becomes “दिनांक १२‑०३‑२०२३” with the date in DD‑MM‑YYYY format using Devanagari digits.

  • Using “निर्णय” for “Award” in labour adjudication — “पंचाट” is the statutory term under the Industrial Disputes Act.
  • Translating “lay‑off” and “retrenchment” both as “छंटनी” — the Act distinguishes “अस्थायी निलंबन” and “सेवा समाप्ति”.
  • Rendering “hereby” as “इसके द्वारा” in Gazette‑level circulars — “एतद्वारा” is the accepted form in delegated legislation.
  • Converting “CEMS”, “EMP”, “ToR” into Hindi words — acronyms are retained in Latin script in official bilingual Gazettes.
  • Using “जुर्माना” for statutory “शास्ति” in SEBI/RBI/Competition Act notifications — “शास्ति” is the prescribed term.
  • Translating “Advance Ruling” as “अग्रिम निर्णय” — the correct term is “अग्रिम विनिर्णय”.
  • Writing numerals in Arabic digits (5%, 180) in Hindi Gazette copy — Devanagari digits (५%, १८०) are mandatory.
The translator who internalises these register‑specific conventions will produce bilingual texts that meet official scrutiny.

Translation of International Arbitration Awards: Enforceability, Reasoning, and Terminology Alignment

International arbitration awards sit at the intersection of procedural law, substantive contract interpretation, and enforcement conventions. The translator must reproduce operative language — “the Tribunal orders”, “the Claimant shall pay”, “the Award is final and binding” — with identical performative force. In English awards, “shall” signals a mandatory directive; in Hindi, “करेगा” (active) or “किया जाएगा” (passive) is chosen based on whether the obligation falls on a party or the award itself. The New York Convention (1958) requires the award to be “binding” and “enforceable”; “बाध्यकारी” is the statutory rendering in the Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996, Section 36. “Final and binding” becomes “अंतिम और बाध्यकारी”.

Reasoning sections (“The Tribunal finds”, “Having regard to”) demand a register mirroring judicial discourse: “अधिकरण का मत है”, “विचार करने पर”. Precedent citations (e.g., “*Bharat Aluminium Co. v. Kaiser Aluminium Technical Services*, (2012) 9 SCC 552”) retain original format; only surrounding text is translated. Technical terms — “seat of arbitration” → “मध्यस्थता का स्थान”, “governing law” → “शासी विधि”, “lex arbitri” → “मध्यस्थता विधि” — are fixed by the Act’s Hindi schedule. The translator must not substitute “कानून” for “विधि” where the Act uses the latter; “विधि” carries the formal legislative nuance required for enforcement under Section 47.

Numbers, dates, and currency follow target-language conventions: Devanagari digits for Hindi (e.g., “USD 1,000,000” → “१०,००,००० अमेरिकी डॉलर”), DD‑MM‑YYYY format with Devanagari digits. “Date of Award” (“Dated this 15th day of March 2024”) becomes “दिनांक १५‑०३‑२०२४ को”. Signature designations (“Presiding Arbitrator”, “Co‑Arbitrator”) use “अध्यक्ष मध्यस्थ”, “सह‑मध्यस्थ” exactly as in the Hindi Arbitration Rules. Consistency across the award, procedural orders, and enforcement petition is essential; divergence creates a “register breach” courts may treat as grounds for setting aside under Section 34.

Translation of Data Protection and Privacy Regulations: Consent, Processing, and Cross‑Border Transfer Clauses

Regimes such as the EU GDPR (2016/679) and India’s DPDP Act, 2023 share a core vocabulary requiring terminological precision. “Personal data” → “व्यक्तिगत डेटा”, “sensitive personal data” → “संवेदनशील व्यक्तिगत डेटा”, “data controller” → “डेटा नियंत्रक”, “data processor” → “डेटा संसाधक”. GDPR’s “lawful basis for processing” (Article 6) → “प्रसंस्करण के लिए विधिक आधार”; DPDP Act uses “विधिक आधार” in Section 4. “Consent” → “सहमति” in both, but GDPR’s “freely given, specific, informed and unambiguous” → “स्वतंत्र, विशिष्ट, सूचित और अस्पष्ट नहीं” — the adjectival chain must be preserved without collapse.

Cross‑border mechanisms — “adequacy decision”, “standard contractual clauses”, “binding corporate rules” — have official Hindi equivalents in DPDP Rules: “पर्याप्तता निर्णय”, “मानक संविदात्मक खंड”, “बाध्यकारी कॉर्पोरेट नियम”. “Transfer to a third country” → “किसी तीसरे देश को अंतरण”. GDPR’s “appropriate safeguards” (Article 46) → “उपयुक्त सुरक्षा उपाय”; DPDP Section 16 uses “समुचित सुरक्षा उपाय”. The translator must match exact statutory phrasing; substituting “उचित” for “समुचित” alters the legal threshold.

Breach timelines (“without undue delay”, “within 72 hours”) → “बिना अनावश्यक विलंब के”, “७२ घंटों के भीतर” (Devanagari digits). “Data protection impact assessment” (DPIA) → “डेटा संरक्षण प्रभाव आकलन”. “Data Protection Officer” → “डेटा संरक्षण अधिकारी”. “Right to erasure” (“right to be forgotten”) → “मिटाने का अधिकार”; “right to data portability” → “डेटा पोर्टेबिलिटी का अधिकार”. Consistency across regulation, rules, and guidance circulars keeps Hindi compliance checklists legally operative.

Back‑translation verification for arbitration award operative paragraphs
An English award reads: “The Tribunal orders the Respondent to pay the Claimant USD 2,500,000 as damages, together with interest at 6% per annum from the date of award until full payment.” Translate into Hindi, then back‑translate to English. Identify any loss of performative force or numeric ambiguity.
Hindi: “अधिकरण प्रतिवादी को आदेश देता है कि वह वादी को २५,००,००० अमेरिकी डॉलर क्षतिपूर्ति के रूप में, साथ ही ६% वार्षिक ब्याज, पंचाट की तारीख से पूर्ण भुगतान तक अदा करे।” Back‑translation: “The Tribunal orders the Respondent to pay the Claimant 2,500,000 US dollars as damages, together with 6% annual interest from the date of award until full payment.” The performative “orders” → “आदेश देता है” retains mandatory force. “Shall pay” → “अदा करे” (active) matches party obligation. Numeric “2,500,000” rendered with Devanagari digits and Indian grouping (२५,००,०००) avoids ambiguity. “Per annum” → “वार्षिक” preserves the interest basis. No loss detected.
Exam‑day strategy
When a translation question presents an arbitration award or data‑protection clause, first isolate performative verbs (order, direct, shall, must). Map each to the target‑language modal carrying identical legal force (करेगा, किया जाएगा, आदेश देता है). Then verify every statutory term against the official Hindi schedule of the governing Act. Finally, run a quick mental back‑translation; any shift in obligation or numeric format signals a register breach examiners penalise.

Translation of Financial Regulatory Circulars: Capital Adequacy, Prudential Norms, and Reporting Formats

RBI circulars on capital adequacy (Basel III), asset classification, and provisioning use a controlled lexicon. “Capital adequacy ratio” → “पूंजी पर्याप्तता अनुपात”, “Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1)” → “कॉमन इक्विटी टियर १ (CET1)”, “Risk‑weighted assets” → “जोखिम‑भारित आस्तियां”. “Minimum CET1 ratio of 5.5%” → “न्यूनतम CET1 अनुपात ५.५%” (Devanagari digits, dot retained). “Leverage ratio” → “लीवरेज अनुपात”, “Liquidity coverage ratio (LCR)” → “लिक्विडिटी कवरेज अनुपात (LCR)”.

Asset classifications — “standard”, “sub‑standard”, “doubtful”, “loss” → “मानक”, “उप‑मानक”, “संदिग्ध”, “हानि”. Provisioning percentages follow the same numeric convention. “The bank shall maintain” → “बैंक बनाए रखेगा” (active, obligation on bank). “The provision shall be made” → “प्रावधान किया जाएगा” (passive, procedural). Reporting formats (e.g., “Form A – Quarterly Return on Capital Adequacy”) keep form identifier in Latin script; title translated: “फॉर्म ए – पूंजी पर्याप्तता पर तिमाही विवरणी”.

Cross‑references to Banking Regulation Act, 1949 (“Section 35A”) → “धारा ३५क”. “In exercise of the powers conferred by Section 35A” → “धारा ३५क द्वारा प्रदत्त शक्तियों का प्रयोग करते हुए”. “Hereby” → “एतद्वारा”. “This circular shall come into force with immediate effect” → “यह परिपत्र तत्काल प्रभाव से लागू होगा”. The translator must preserve “shall” (obligation → “करेगा/किया जाएगा”) vs. “will” (future fact → “होगा”).

Translation of Public Health Emergency Orders: Quarantine, Vaccination, and Reporting Obligations

Orders under the Epidemic Diseases Act, 1897, Disaster Management Act, 2005, and National Health Mission guidelines use a directive register leaving no ambiguity. “Quarantine” → “संगरोध”, “isolation” → “अलगाव”, “containment zone” → “नियंत्रण क्षेत्र”. “Mandatory quarantine for 14 days” → “१४ दिन का अनिवार्य संगरोध”. “Vaccination certificate” → “टीकाकरण प्रमाणपत्र”, “adverse event following immunization (AEFI)” → “टीकाकरणोत्तर प्रतिकूल घटना (AEFI)”.

Reporting obligations — “All hospitals shall report suspected cases within 24 hours” → “सभी अस्पताल २४ घंटों के भीतर संदिग्ध मामलों की सूचना देंगे” (“शall” → “देंगे”, active future, duty on hospital). “The District Magistrate may issue directions” → “जिला मजिस्ट्रेट निर्देश जारी कर सकेगा” (“may” → “सकेगा”, permissive). “Non‑compliance shall attract penal action under Section 188 IPC” → “अनुपालन न करने पर भा.दं.सं. की धारा १८८ के तहत दंडात्मक कार्रवाई की जाएगी” (passive “की जाएगी” preserves procedural nature).

Vaccine names — “Covishield”, “Covaxin”, “Sputnik V” — remain in Latin script; Hindi adds generic “टीका” after first mention (“कोविशील्ड टीका”). Dates use Devanagari digits, DD‑MM‑YYYY. “With immediate effect” → “तत्काल प्रभाव से”. “This order shall remain in force until further orders” → “यह आदेश आगामी आदेशों तक लागू रहेगा”. The Hindi version must mirror the English temporal scope exactly; shifts (e.g., “until further notice” → “आगामी सूचना तक”) change the legal endpoint and may be challenged.

Translation of Legislative Interpretation Clauses: Definitions, Commencement, and Repeal Provisions

Every statute contains an interpretation clause (often Section 2) defining terms for the entire Act. The translator must treat these definitions as “locked” — the Hindi equivalent is authoritative for all later references. The Companies Act, 2013 defines “company” as “कंपनी”, “private company” as “निजी कंपनी”. “Officer” includes “any director, manager or secretary” → “कोई निदेशक, प्रबंधक या सचिव”. “Unless the context otherwise requires” → “जब तक संदर्भ से अन्यथा अपेक्षित न हो”. This clause governs every later occurrence; substituting “अधिकारी” for “ऑफिसर” creates terminological drift.

Commencement provisions (“This Act shall come into force on such date as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, appoint”) follow a fixed pattern: “यह अधिनियम उस तारीख को लागू होगा जो केंद्रीय सरकार राजपत्र में अधिसूचना द्वारा नियत करे”. “By notification in the Official Gazette” → “राजपत्र में अधिसूचना द्वारा”. “Appoint” → “नियत करे” (for dates), not “नियुक्त करे” (for persons).

Repeal and savings clauses (“The enactments specified in the Schedule are hereby repealed”) use “एतद्वारा” for “hereby”. “Repealed” → “निरस्त”, “Savings” → “बचाव”. “Nothing in this Act shall affect any right, privilege, obligation or liability acquired, accrued or incurred before the commencement” → “इस अधिनियम की कोई बात लागू होने से पहले अर्जित, उपचित या उत्पन्न किसी अधिकार, विशेषाधिकार, दायित्व या देयता को प्रभावित नहीं करेगी”. “Shall not affect” → “प्रभावित नहीं करेगी”. The scope of “acquired, accrued or incurred” — “अर्जित, उपचित या उत्पन्न” — must be preserved; each term has distinct meaning in limitation jurisprudence.

  • Using “कानून” for “विधि” in arbitration awards — the Arbitration Act reserves “विधि” for statutory law; “कानून” is generic and weakens enforceability.
  • Translating “shall” uniformly as “होगा” — active party obligations require “करेगा”; procedural passives need “किया जाएगा”.
  • Rendering “adequacy decision” as “पर्याप्त निर्णय” instead of statutory “पर्याप्तता निर्णय” — the suffix “ता” marks the legal concept.
  • Writing percentages with Arabic digits (5.5%) in Hindi Gazette copy — Devanagari digits (५.५%) are mandatory.
  • Substituting “नियुक्त करे” for “नियत करे” in commencement clauses — “नियत” applies to dates, “नियुक्त” to persons.
  • Collapsing “freely given, specific, informed and unambiguous” into a single adjective — GDPR and DPDP Act require each qualifier distinct.
  • Translating “hereby” as “इसके द्वारा” in delegated legislation — “एतद्वारा” is the prescribed form.

Translation of Constitutional Amendment Texts and Basic Structure Jurisprudence

Constitutional amendment provisions require strict terminological fidelity because every word becomes part of the supreme law. The phrase “basic structure” (मूल ढांचा) was articulated in Kesavananda Bharati v. State of Kerala (1973); official Hindi texts consistently use “मूल ढांचा” rather than “मूल संरचना” or “आधारभूत ढांचा”. The amendment formula “Parliament may, by law, amend any provision of this Constitution” translates to “संसद, विधि द्वारा, इस संविधान के किसी उपबंध का संशोधन कर सकेगी”. The modal “may” → “सकेगी” preserves the permissive character; “shall” in the corresponding procedural clause (“The Bill shall be passed by each House”) becomes “पारित किया जाएगा” (passive) to reflect mandatory legislative process.

Article 368’s marginal note “Power of Parliament to amend the Constitution and procedure therefor” is rendered “संविधान का संशोधन करने की संसद की शक्ति और उसकी प्रक्रिया”. The term “procedure therefor” → “उसकी प्रक्रिया” avoids the calque “उसके लिए प्रक्रिया”. In the proviso “Provided that no amendment shall be called in question in any court on the ground that it violates the basic structure”, the negative “no amendment shall be called in question” → “कोई संशोधन किसी न्यायालय में इस आधार पर प्रश्नगत नहीं किया जाएगा कि वह मूल ढांचे का उल्लंघन करता है”. The phrase “called in question” is a legal term of art; “प्रश्नगत किया जाएगा” is the standard forensic equivalent.

In Hindi statutory drafting, the verb “उल्लंघन करता है” treats the amendment as the agent in a direct-case construction; translators should not insert an ergative marker “ने”. Consistency across amendment acts (e.g., the 42nd, 44th, 86th Amendments) is maintained through standardized terminology adopted by the Legislative Department.

Key Terminology Lock‑In

  • Basic structure → मूल ढांचा
  • Amendment → संशोधन
  • Proviso → परंतुक
  • Called in question → प्रश्नगत किया जाना
  • Violation → उल्लंघन

Translation of Judicial Review Standards: Proportionality, Wednesbury, and Legitimate Expectation

Judicial review vocabulary is a closed set where each English term maps to a single Hindi counterpart carrying the same doctrinal weight. “Proportionality” → “आनुपातिकता” (not “समानुपातिकता”). The four‑step test — legitimate aim, rational connection, necessity, balancing — is commonly rendered as “वैध उद्देश्य, युक्तियुक्त संबंध, आवश्यकता, और संतुलन”. “Wednesbury unreasonableness” (from Associated Provincial Picture Houses Ltd v. Wednesbury Corporation 1948) becomes “वेडनेस्बरी असंगतता”. The phrase “so unreasonable that no reasonable authority could have come to it” → “इतनी असंगत कि कोई युक्तियुक्त प्राधिकरण उस तक नहीं पहुंच सकता था”. “Legitimate expectation” → “वैध अपेक्षा”. The procedural legitimate expectation (“a promise or practice”) → “आश्वासन या प्रथा”; substantive legitimate expectation (“a benefit”) → “लाभ”.

In orders, the court often writes “The decision fails the proportionality test” → “निर्णय आनुपातिकता की कसौटी पर विफल रहता है”. “Fails” → “विफल रहता है” (present habitual) rather than “विफल हुआ” (past) because the judicial pronouncement speaks in the timeless present. “The authority acted Wednesbury‑unreasonably” → “प्राधिकरण ने वेडनेस्बरी‑असंगततापूर्वक कार्य किया”. The hyphenated compound preserves the eponymous label; splitting it into “वेडनेस्बरी असंगततापूर्वक” is acceptable but the hyphen signals a single doctrinal concept.

When the judgment quotes “legitimate expectation arises from a clear, unambiguous promise”, the translation must keep the adjectives distinct: “स्पष्ट, असंदिग्ध आश्वासन”. Collapsing them into “स्पष्ट आश्वासन” loses the doctrinal nuance that ambiguity defeats the expectation. The phrase “procedural fairness” → “प्रक्रियात्मक निष्पक्षता”; “substantive fairness” → “मूलभूत निष्पक्षता”. The distinction mirrors the English split and is required in administrative law syllabi.

Translation Exercise
Translate the following paragraph into Hindi, preserving doctrinal terminology and register: “The High Court held that the impugned order fails the proportionality test because the restriction imposed is not necessary in a democratic society. The authority’s decision is Wednesbury‑unreasonable, and the applicant’s legitimate expectation of a hearing was disregarded.”
उच्च न्यायालय ने अभिनिर्धारित किया कि विवादित आदेश आनुपातिकता की कसौटी पर विफल रहता है क्योंकि आरोपित प्रतिबंध एक लोकतांत्रिक समाज में आवश्यक नहीं है। प्राधिकरण का निर्णय वेडनेस्बरी‑असंगततापूर्वक है, और आवेदक की सुनवाई की वैध अपेक्षा की अवहेलना की गई।
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the locked Hindi equivalents for the three standards (आनुपातिकता, वेडनेस्बरी‑असंगतता, वैध अपेक्षा). In the exam, underline the English term in the source text, then write the Hindi term directly above it; this prevents accidental synonym substitution under time pressure.

Translation of Administrative Law Concepts: Ultra Vires, Natural Justice, and Reasoned Decisions

Administrative law translation hinges on three pillars: ultra vires, natural justice, and the duty to give reasons. “Ultra vires” (beyond powers) → “अधिकारातीत”. The phrase “The order is ultra vires the enabling statute” → “आदेश सक्षम विधि के अधिकारातीत है”. The preposition “of” in “ultra vires of” is rendered by the postposition “के” after the statute name. “Intra vires” → “अधिकारान्तर्गत”. “Natural justice” → “प्राकृतिक न्याय”. Its two limbs — “audi alteram partem” (hear the other side) and “nemo judex in causa sua” (no one should be a judge in his own cause) — translate as “दूसरे पक्ष को सुनना” and “कोई अपने मामले में न्यायाधीश नहीं हो सकता”. In official orders, the Latin maxims are often retained in parentheses: “प्राकृतिक न्याय (audi alteram partem)”.

“Reasoned decision” → “तर्कसंगत निर्णय”. The statutory requirement “The authority shall record reasons for its decision” → “प्राधिकरण अपने निर्णय के कारण अभिलिखित करेगा”. “Record reasons” → “कारण अभिलिखित करना” (not “लिखना”). “Speaking order” (an order that speaks for itself) → “स्वयंभाषी आदेश”. The phrase “The order is a speaking order” → “आदेश एक स्वयंभाषी आदेश है”. “Non‑speaking order” → “गैर‑स्वयंभाषी आदेश”.

When translating “The principle of legitimate expectation is a facet of natural justice”, the word “facet” → “पहलू”. “The doctrine of proportionality is an aspect of judicial review” → “आनुपातिकता का सिद्धांत न्यायिक पुनर्विलोकन का एक पहलू है”. Consistency in using “पहलू” for both “facet” and “aspect” prevents terminological drift across the same judgment.

Common Collocations

  • Ultra vires the Act → अधिनियम के अधिकारातीत
  • Breach of natural justice → प्राकृतिक न्याय का उल्लंघन
  • Duty to give reasons → कारण देने का कर्तव्य
  • Speaking order → स्वयंभाषी आदेश

Translation of International Human Rights Treaty Body Concluding Observations

Concluding observations of UN treaty bodies (e.g., Human Rights Committee, Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights) follow a rigid template: “The Committee welcomes…”, “The Committee is concerned…”, “The Committee recommends…”. The Hindi equivalents are fixed: “समिति स्वागत करती है…”, “समिति चिंतित है…”, “समिति सिफारिश करती है…”. The modal “should” in recommendations → “चाहिए” (e.g., “The State party should ensure…” → “राज्य पक्ष को सुनिश्चित करना चाहिए…”). “Shall” in obligations → “करेगा/किया जाएगा” depending on agency.

Key thematic vocabulary: “non‑discrimination” → “गैर‑भेदभाव”; “equality before the law” → “कानून के समक्ष समानता”; “effective remedy” → “प्रभावी उपचार”; “progressive realization” → “क्रमिक प्राप्ति”; “minimum core obligations” → “न्यूनतम मूल दायित्व”. The phrase “The Committee notes with concern the persistent discrimination against…” → “समिति … के विरुद्ध सतत भेदभाव पर चिंता व्यक्त करती है”. “Persistent” → “सतत” or “लगातार”; “discrimination” → “भेदभाव”. “Notes with concern” is a set phrase; the idiomatic “चिंता व्यक्त करती है” is preferred in official Hindi versions over the literal “चिंता के साथ नोट करती है”.

Paragraph numbering and cross‑references must be preserved exactly: “Paragraph 12” → “पैराग्राफ १२”. Footnote markers (e.g., “[1]”) remain unchanged. When the observation cites a previous concluding observation (“see CCPR/C/XYZ/CO/5, para. 8”), the citation is kept in Latin script; the Hindi text adds “देखें” before it.

Register Calibration

The register is formal, impersonal, and hortatory. Passive constructions dominate: “It is recommended that…” → “यह सिफारिश की जाती है कि…”. The translator must avoid active “हम सिफारिश करते हैं” because the Committee speaks as an institutional voice, not as individuals.

Translation of Cyber Law and Data Localization Provisions

Cyber legislation (e.g., Information Technology Act, 2000; Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023) introduces a technical lexicon that must be rendered with statutory precision. “Data localization” → “डेटा स्थानीयकरण”. “Cross‑border data transfer” → “सीमा‑पार डेटा अंतरण”. “Data fiduciary” → “डेटा न्यासी”. “Data principal” → “डेटा प्रधान”. “Consent manager” → “सहमति प्रबंधक”. “Significant data fiduciary” → “महत्वपूर्ण डेटा न्यासी”. The phrase “The data fiduciary shall store a copy of personal data within India” → “डेटा न्यासी व्यक्तिगत डेटा की एक प्रति भारत के भीतर संग्रहीत करेगा”. “Shall” → “करेगा” (active obligation).

“Processing” → “प्रसंस्करण”. “Automated decision‑making” → “स्वचालित निर्णय‑निर्माण”. “Profiling” → “प्रोफाइलिंग”. “Data breach” → “डेटा उल्लंघन”. “Data protection impact assessment (DPIA)” → “डेटा संरक्षण प्रभाव आकलन (DPIA)”. The acronym DPIA is retained in Latin script; the Hindi expansion follows in parentheses.

Penal provisions: “Whoever contravenes… shall be punishable with imprisonment up to three years” → “जो कोई उल्लंघन करेगा… उसे तीन वर्ष तक के कारावास से दंडित किया जाएगा”. “Contravenes” → “उल्लंघन करेगा”. “Punishable with” → “से दंडित किया जाएगा”. The passive “दंडित किया जाएगा” mirrors the English passive “shall be punishable”.

Regulatory powers: “The Central Government may, by notification, specify categories of data fiduciaries” → “केंद्रीय सरकार, अधिसूचना द्वारा, डेटा न्यासियों की श्रेणियां विनिर्दिष्ट कर सकेगी”. “May” → “सकेगी”. “Specify” → “विनिर्दिष्ट करना”. “Notification” → “अधिसूचना”. The phrase “by notification” is a fixed collocation; “अधिसूचना द्वारा” is the statutory norm.

Terminology Consistency Checklist

  • Data fiduciary → डेटा न्यासी (never डेटा न्यासीगण)
  • Data principal → डेटा प्रधान
  • Consent manager → सहमति प्रबंधक
  • Cross‑border transfer → सीमा‑पार अंतरण
  • DPIA → DPIA (डेटा संरक्षण प्रभाव आकलन)
  • Using “मूल संरचना” for “basic structure” — the locked term is “मूल ढांचा”.
  • Rendering “proportionality” as “समानुपातिकता” instead of “आनुपातिकता”.
  • Translating “Wednesbury unreasonableness” as “वेडनेस्बरी अनुचितता” — the doctrinal label is “वेडनेस्बरी‑असंगतता”.
  • Collapsing “legitimate expectation” into “उचित अपेक्षा” — the correct term is “वैध अपेक्षा”.
  • Writing “अधिकारातीत” as “अधिकारों से परे” — the legal term is a single word.
  • Using “प्राकृतिक न्याय” for “natural justice” but omitting the Latin maxims in parentheses when the source includes them.
  • Translating “data fiduciary” as “डेटा न्यासीगण” or “डेटा न्यासीजन” — the statute uses the singular form as a defined term.
The sections above equip the aspirant with the precise terminology, register controls, and structural awareness required for high‑stakes translation tasks in compulsory language papers.

Translation of Scientific Research Abstracts: Structured Move Analysis

Scientific abstracts follow a conventional rhetorical structure often described as the IMRaD move sequence: Background, Purpose, Method, Results, and Conclusion. In translation, each move must be rendered with the same information density and logical flow as the source. The Background move typically employs present perfect or present tense to situate the study (“Recent studies have shown…”). The Purpose move uses infinitival or modal constructions (“This paper aims to…”, “We investigate…”). The Method move favours past tense passive (“Samples were collected…”) while the Results move mixes past tense for specific findings (“The treatment reduced…”) and present tense for generalisable statements (“These findings suggest…”). The Conclusion move often returns to present perfect or modal forms (“The study has demonstrated…”, “Further research should…”).

When translating into a target language that marks evidentiality or aspect differently, the translator must preserve the epistemic stance of each move. For example, Hindi uses the perfective aspect “हुआ है” for completed actions and the habitual “होता है” for general truths; mapping the English present perfect “has shown” to “दिखाया है” retains the evidential weight. In languages with a dedicated inferential evidential (e.g., Turkish “-miş”), the translator may need to add a particle to signal that the background claim is reported rather than directly observed.

Terminological consistency across moves is critical. A term introduced in the Background (“nanoparticle”) must appear unchanged in Method, Results, and Conclusion. Glossaries compiled from the source journal’s author guidelines (e.g., IEEE, Elsevier) should be consulted before translation begins. Numerical values, units, and statistical symbols (p‑value, CI, SD) are retained in Latin script per international convention; only the surrounding prose is translated.

Move boundaries are often signalled by discourse markers (“However,”, “Moreover,”, “In conclusion,”). These markers must be rendered with equivalents that preserve the same cohesive function. In French, “Toutefois” and “En conclusion” serve the same rhetorical purpose; in Japanese, sentence‑final particles “が” and “ため” can indicate contrast and purpose respectively. Avoid inserting additional markers that alter the move structure.

Finally, length constraints imposed by target journals (often 250‑300 words) require condensation without loss of moves. Strategies include nominalisation (“The analysis of data revealed…” → “Data analysis revealed…”) and omission of redundant hedges, provided the epistemic stance remains intact.

Handling Ambiguity in Patent Claims and Technical Specifications

Patent claims are drafted to maximise scope while satisfying the definiteness requirement of Article 84 EPC and 35 U.S.C. § 112. Ambiguity arises from broad functional language (“means for…”), relative terms (“substantially”, “approximately”), and nested dependent claims. The translator must preserve the legal effect of each claim element: a “means‑plus‑function” limitation in the source triggers a corresponding structure in the specification; the translation must retain the “means for” phrasing or its jurisdictional equivalent (e.g., “Mittel zur” in German, “手段” in Japanese).

Relative terms are interpreted by courts with reference to the specification and prosecution history. When translating “substantially uniform”, the target language should use the term the local patent office recognises as a term of art — “im Wesentlichen gleichmäßig” (DE), “実質的に均一” (JP). Substituting a literal “लगभग समान” in Hindi would shift the claim scope and risk invalidation.

Dependent claims incorporate all limitations of the parent claim. The translation must maintain the hierarchical numbering and cross‑references (“claim 1”, “claim 2”) exactly; any renumbering breaks the legal chain. In bilingual filings (e.g., PCT applications), the English version is often the authentic text; the translation is for information only. Nevertheless, national phase entry may require a certified translation that becomes the basis for examination. Errors in the certified translation can be cited as grounds for amendment or rejection.

Technical specifications accompanying claims (drawings, embodiments, examples) use a controlled vocabulary defined in the description. The translator should build a termbase from the description’s “Definitions” section and apply it consistently across claims, description, and drawings. For instance, “substrate” must not become “base layer” in one place and “foundation” in another. The termbase should also capture measurement units (µm, nm, °C) and reference signs (10, 12a) which remain unchanged.

Procedural language in the specification (“The invention relates to…”, “According to an embodiment…”) follows formulaic patterns. In French, “L’invention concerne…” and “Selon un mode de réalisation…” are fixed equivalents. Deviating from these formulas can cause the examiner to treat the text as a new disclosure, affecting priority.

Worked Translation of a Patent Claim
Translate the following independent claim into Hindi, preserving claim scope and terminology: “A device for wireless power transmission, comprising: a transmitter coil configured to generate an alternating magnetic field; a receiver coil configured to induce an alternating current in response to the alternating magnetic field; and a control unit configured to adjust the frequency of the alternating magnetic field based on a feedback signal from the receiver coil.”
वायरलेस विद्युत संचरण के लिए एक उपकरण, जिसमें शामिल हैं: एक ट्रांसमीटर कॉइल जो एक प्रत्यावर्ती चुंबकीय क्षेत्र उत्पन्न करने के लिए विन्यस्त है; एक रिसीवर कॉइल जो प्रत्यावर्ती चुंबकीय क्षेत्र की प्रतिक्रिया में एक प्रत्यावर्ती धारा प्रेरित करने के लिए विन्यस्त है; और एक नियंत्रण इकाई जो रिसीवर कॉइल से एक फीडबैक सिग्नल के आधार पर प्रत्यावर्ती चुंबकीय क्षेत्र की आवृत्ति को समायोजित करने के लिए विन्यस्त है।

Key decisions: “device” → “उपकरण” (standard patent term); “configured to” → “के लिए विन्यस्त है” (preserves functional limitation); “alternating magnetic field” → “प्रत्यावर्ती चुंबकीय क्षेत्र” (technical term); “induce” → “प्रेरित करना” (physics terminology); “feedback signal” → “फीडबैक सिग्नल” (retain English term as per Indian Patent Office practice); “adjust the frequency” → “आवृत्ति को समायोजित करना”. No claim numbering altered; all commas and semicolons retained to preserve claim structure.
Exam‑Day Strategy for Patent Translation
Before the exam, memorise the fixed equivalents for the ten most common claim‑drafting formulas in the target language (e.g., “comprising”, “configured to”, “means for”, “wherein”, “said”). During the test, underline every claim element in the source, then translate element‑by‑element using the memorised formulas. This prevents accidental scope changes and saves time on re‑reading.

Translating Statistical Tables, Graph Captions, and Data Visualisations

Statistical tables in official reports (e.g., Census, NSSO, WHO) follow a rigid layout: title, column headings, stub headings, body cells, footnotes, and source line. The title is a noun phrase summarising the variable, period, and geography (“Table 1: Literacy Rate by State, 2021”). In translation, the title must retain the same syntactic order; reordering for target‑language word order can misalign cross‑references in the text (“as shown in Table 1”). Column headings often contain abbreviations (M, F, UR, R) defined in a footnote; these abbreviations are kept in Latin script, while the footnote expansion is translated.

Graph captions describe the visual encoding: “Figure 2: Bar chart showing GDP growth (%) for selected economies, 2010‑2020”. The caption’s prepositional phrases (“for”, “from”, “by”) map to case markers or postpositions in the target language. The translator must not convert the chart type (“bar chart”) into a generic “graph” because the visual form is part of the evidential claim.

Numeric values, units, and symbols (%, ₹, °C, km²) remain unchanged. Decimal separators follow the target locale (comma in many European languages, point in English). However, in bilingual official publications the source locale is often preserved to avoid misreading; the translator should follow the publishing authority’s style guide.

Footnotes attached to specific cells (e.g., “a Data for 2020 are provisional”) use superscript letters or symbols. The footnote marker stays identical; the footnote text is translated. When a footnote references a methodological note (“See Technical Appendix A”), the cross‑reference is kept in Latin script with a translated preface (“देखें तकनीकी परिशिष्ट A”).

Data visualisations such as heat maps, choropleths, and Sankey diagrams carry legends with colour‑scale labels (“Low”, “Medium”, “High”). These labels are translated, but the colour‑scale order must not be altered. In languages with grammatical gender, the adjective forms must agree with the noun they modify in the legend (e.g., Spanish “Bajo”, “Medio”, “Alto”).

Finally, accessibility requirements (alt‑text for screen readers) demand a concise verbal description of the visual. The alt‑text translation must convey the same quantitative insight (e.g., “Bar chart: GDP growth rose from 6.1 % in 2019 to 8.3 % in 2021”) without reproducing the entire table.

Managing Terminology in Multilingual Legislative Drafts and EU Directives

EU directives are drafted simultaneously in 24 official languages; each language version is equally authentic. The translation unit (usually the Directorate‑General for Translation) follows the “Interinstitutional Style Guide” and the “EuroVoc” thesaurus for concept‑level alignment. A translator working on a national implementation must mirror the EU‑level term choices to avoid divergence. For example, “data controller” is fixed as “डेटा नियंत्रक” in Hindi EuroVoc; using “डेटा प्रबंधक” would create a non‑standard term.

Legislative drafts at the national level often incorporate EU‑derived definitions verbatim. The translator must locate the source directive’s article and recital numbers, then apply the exact target‑language equivalents from the published Official Journal. Recitals, which provide interpretive context, use a distinct rhetorical style: “Having regard to…”, “Considering that…”. In Hindi, these become “…को ध्यान में रखते हुए” and “यह विचार करते हुए कि…”. The translator must preserve the recital numbering and the logical connectors (“whereas”, “therefore”) because courts refer to recitals for purposive interpretation.

Definitions sections list terms in alphabetical order with a colon and a defining clause (“‘personal data’ means any information relating to an identified or identifiable natural person”). The colon is retained; the defining clause is translated preserving the logical structure (“‘व्यक्तिगत डेटा’ का अर्थ है किसी पहचाने गए या पहचाने जाने योग्य प्राकृतिक व्यक्ति से संबंधित कोई भी जानकारी”). The term itself is placed in single quotation marks in the target script if the target language uses them; otherwise, the convention of the Official Journal (e.g., guillemets « » in French) is followed.

Amendments and corrigenda are issued as separate instruments (“Regulation (EU) 2023/1234 amending Regulation (EU) 2016/679”). The translator must translate the amending instrument’s title, citation, and the exact textual modifications (insertions, deletions, replacements). The modification language (“Article 5 is replaced by the following:”) uses a formulaic pattern that has a fixed equivalent in each language (“अनुच्छेद 5 को निम्नलिखित द्वारा प्रतिस्थापित किया जाता है:”).

Consistency across the entire legislative corpus is enforced by terminology databases (e.g., IATE). The translator should run a terminology check against IATE before finalising the draft. Any deviation must be justified in a translation note, which becomes part of the legislative record.

Strategies for Translating Diplomatic Notes, Aide‑Mémoire, and Formal Correspondence

Diplomatic notes follow a highly ritualised structure: heading (Note Verbale, Note, Aide‑Mémoire), reference number, date, salutation, body paragraphs numbered consecutively, complimentary close, and signature block. The heading determines the level of formality; a Note Verbale is third‑person (“The Embassy of… presents its compliments to the Ministry of… and has the honour to inform…”). In translation, the third‑person voice must be retained; shifting to first person (“We inform…”) alters the diplomatic persona.

Standardised phrases (“has the honour to”, “avails itself of this opportunity to renew the assurances of its highest consideration”) have standard renderings in each language. In Hindi, “अपनी हार्दिक शुभकामनाओं के साथ” and “अवसर का लाभ उठाते हुए” are accepted equivalents. The translator must not paraphrase these formulae; they are part of the diplomatic code and their omission signals a breach of protocol.

Reference numbers (e.g., “Ref: 2024/045/POL”) and dates (DD Month YYYY) are copied unchanged. The date format may be adapted to the target locale (e.g., १५ जनवरी २०२४) but the numeric values remain identical.

Body paragraphs often contain conditional commitments (“Should the Government of… decide to… the Embassy would…”). The conditional mood must be mapped to the target language’s equivalent (Hindi “यदि … निर्णय लेता है तो दूतावास … करेगा”). The use of “would” versus “will” distinguishes a polite offer from a firm undertaking; the translation must preserve this nuance.

Aide‑Mémoire are less formal but still structured: title, summary of issues, proposed actions, and a closing paragraph requesting a response. They frequently embed bullet points or numbered lists. The translator must keep the list formatting and the imperative mood of proposed actions (“Request the Ministry to…”, “Propose that…”). In Hindi, the honorific imperative “कृपया … करें” is used for requests, while “प्रस्ताव है कि…” introduces proposals.

Signature blocks include the sender’s name, title, and diplomatic rank. Titles such as “Chargé d’Affaires a.i.” have established translations (“कार्यवाहक राजदूत”). The translator must not invent a translation; the Ministry of External Affairs maintains a glossary of diplomatic titles.

  • Replacing fixed diplomatic formulae with paraphrases, which changes the legal‑diplomatic effect of the note.
  • Altering the third‑person voice of a Note Verbale to first person, thereby misrepresenting the sending mission’s stance.
  • Translating reference numbers or dates into a different numeric system without preserving the original characters.
  • Using a generic term for a defined diplomatic title instead of the authorised glossary entry.
  • Changing the conditional mood (“would”) to indicative (“will”) in commitments, turning a courteous offer into a binding promise.
  • Omitting the complimentary close or substituting an informal closing, which breaches protocol.
  • Reordering numbered paragraphs in the body, breaking cross‑references in subsequent correspondence.

These sections equip the translator with the precise mechanisms, exceptions, and examiner‑focused pitfalls needed for high‑stakes compulsory language papers.

Translation of Federalism and Distribution of Powers Texts: Seventh Schedule, Repugnancy, and Article 254

Texts governing the distribution of legislative powers between the Union and the States — primarily the Seventh Schedule (Union List, State List, Concurrent List), Article 246, and the repugnancy clause in Article 254 — demand terminological precision because a single mistranslated term can alter the constitutional balance. The three Lists contain entries drafted in broad, overlapping language (e.g., Entry 23 Union List: “Social security and social insurance; employment and unemployment” versus Entry 24 State List: “Relief of the disabled and unemployable”). The translator must preserve the exact phrasing of each entry; paraphrasing “social security” as “सामाजिक सुरक्षा” is standard, but rendering “unemployment” as “बेरोज़गारी” in one entry and “रोज़गारहीनता” in another creates inadvertent divergence. Article 254(1) states that if a State law is repugnant to a Union law on a Concurrent subject, the Union law prevails “whether passed before or after the State law”. The temporal scope (“whether passed before or after”) must be rendered without ambiguity; in Hindi, “चाहे वह राज्य विधि के पहले पारित की गई हो या बाद में” captures the bidirectional reach. Article 254(2) introduces the Presidential assent exception: a State law reserved for the President’s consideration and receiving assent prevails in that State “notwithstanding anything in this Constitution”. The phrase “notwithstanding anything in this Constitution” is a constitutional override formula; its standard Hindi equivalent “इस संविधान में किसी बात के होते हुए भी” must be used verbatim. The translator must also distinguish between “repugnancy” (direct conflict making simultaneous obedience impossible) and “occupying the field” (Union law so comprehensive it leaves no room for State legislation). The Supreme Court in M. Karunanidhi v. Union of India (1979) and Zameer Ahmed Latifur Rehman Sheikh v. State of Maharashtra (2010) articulated these tests; translations of judicial reasoning on repugnancy must retain the analytical vocabulary: “direct inconsistency”, “irreconcilable conflict”, “dominance of Union legislation”. Entries in the Seventh Schedule referring to specific statutes (e.g., Entry 52 Union List: “Industries declared by Parliament by law to be necessary for the purpose of defence or for the prosecution of war”) require the translator to track declared industries notifications and use the exact nomenclature from the Gazette. When translating amendments to the Lists (e.g., the 42nd Amendment moving “Education”, “Forests”, “Protection of wild animals and birds” from State to Concurrent List), the amendment instrument’s wording — “Entry 11 shall be omitted”, “after Entry 17, the following Entry shall be inserted” — follows a rigid legislative drafting pattern that must be mirrored in the target language.

Translation of Emergency Provisions and Constitutional Suspension Instruments: Articles 352, 356, and 360

The three emergency provisions — National Emergency (Art. 352), President’s Rule / State Emergency (Art. 356), and Financial Emergency (Art. 360) — generate distinct instrument types: Proclamations, Parliamentary approval resolutions, revocation orders, and judicial review orders. Each carries formulaic language that admits no paraphrase. A Proclamation under Article 352(1) recites “the security of India or any part of the territory thereof is threatened by war or external aggression or armed rebellion”. The three grounds — “war” (युद्ध), “external aggression” (बाह्य आक्रमण), “armed rebellion” (सशस्त्र विद्रोह) — are terms of art; “armed rebellion” replaced “internal disturbance” by the 44th Amendment (1978), and the translator must never substitute the older term. The Proclamation must specify whether it extends to the whole of India or a part; the phrase “the whole of India or such part of the territory thereof as may be specified” translates as “संपूर्ण भारत या उसके राज्यक्षेत्र का ऐसा भाग जो विनिर्दिष्ट किया जाए”. Article 352(3) requires the Proclamation to be laid before each House of Parliament; the resolution approving it uses the formula “This House approves the Proclamation issued by the President on [date] under Article 352(1)”. In Hindi: “यह सदन राष्ट्रपति द्वारा अनुच्छेद 352(1) के अधीन [तारीख] को जारी उद्घोषणा का अनुमोदन करता है”. The one-month approval window and the requirement of a special majority (majority of total membership and two-thirds of members present and voting) are procedural details that appear in the resolution text and must be preserved. Article 356 Proclamations (“failure of constitutional machinery in a State”) incorporate the Governor’s report; the phrase “a situation has arisen in which the Government of the State cannot be carried on in accordance with the provisions of this Constitution” is the constitutional trigger. The Supreme Court in S.R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994) subjected the subjective satisfaction to judicial review; translations of Bommai reasoning must retain “subjective satisfaction”, “mala fide”, “irrelevant grounds”, “perversity”. The Proclamation under Article 356(1) may “assume to himself all or any of the functions of the Government of the State” and “declare that the powers of the Legislature of the State shall be exercisable by or under the authority of Parliament”. These clauses — “assume to himself”, “exercisable by or under the authority of Parliament” — have fixed Hindi renderings: “अपने ऊपर ले लेना” and “संसद द्वारा या उसके प्राधिकार के अधीन प्रयोग की जाने योग्य”. Article 360 Financial Emergency Proclamations are rare; the trigger is “the financial stability or credit of India or any part of the territory thereof is threatened”. The Proclamation empowers the President to issue directions for reduction of salaries and allowances of all or any class of persons serving in connection with the affairs of the Union including the Judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts. The phrase “persons serving in connection with the affairs of the Union” is a defined category; its translation “संघ के कार्यों के संबंध में सेवा करने वाले व्यक्ति” must remain consistent across all financial emergency instruments.

Worked Translation: Article 254(2) Repugnancy Exception Clause
Translate the following provision into Hindi for a bilingual legislative text: "Notwithstanding anything in this Constitution, a law made by the Legislature of a State with respect to any matter enumerated in the Concurrent List containing any provision repugnant to the provisions of an earlier law made by Parliament or an existing law with respect to that matter, shall, if it has been reserved for the consideration of the President and has received his assent, prevail in that State."
Step 1 — Segment by constitutional formula: "Notwithstanding anything in this Constitution" → "इस संविधान में किसी बात के होते हुए भी" (fixed override formula).
Step 2 — Subject clause: "a law made by the Legislature of a State with respect to any matter enumerated in the Concurrent List" → "राज्य के विधि-मंडल द्वारा समवर्ती सूची में गिनाए गए किसी विषय के संबंध में बनाई गई विधि".
Step 3 — Relative clause (repugnancy): "containing any provision repugnant to the provisions of an earlier law made by Parliament or an existing law with respect to that matter" → "जिसमें संसद द्वारा पहले बनाई गई किसी विधि या उस विषय के संबंध में किसी विद्यमान विधि के उपबंधों के प्रतिकूल कोई उपबंध अंतर्विष्ट हो". Note: "repugnant to" → "प्रतिकूल" (standard legal equivalent); "earlier law" → "पहले बनाई गई विधि"; "existing law" → "विद्यमान विधि" (Art. 366 definition).
Step 4 — Condition clause: "shall, if it has been reserved for the consideration of the President and has received his assent" → "यदि वह राष्ट्रपति के विचारार्थ आरक्षित की गई हो और उसकी अनुमति प्राप्त कर ली गई हो, तो". "Reserved for consideration" → "विचारार्थ आरक्षित की गई हो"; "received his assent" → "अनुमति प्राप्त कर ली गई हो".
Step 5 — Consequence: "prevail in that State" → "उस राज्य में प्रभावी होगी".
Full rendered text: "इस संविधान में किसी बात के होते हुए भी, राज्य के विधि-मंडल द्वारा समवर्ती सूची में गिनाए गए किसी विषय के संबंध में बनाई गई विधि, जिसमें संसद द्वारा पहले बनाई गई किसी विधि या उस विषय के संबंध में किसी विद्यमान विधि के उपबंधों के प्रतिकूल कोई उपबंध अंतर्विष्ट हो, यदि वह राष्ट्रपति के विचारार्थ आरक्षित की गई हो और उसकी अनुमति प्राप्त कर ली गई हो, तो उस राज्य में प्रभावी होगी."
Exam-Day Strategy: Constitutional Formula Lock-In
Memorise the exact Hindi/English equivalents for the 12 most frequent constitutional override and procedural formulae: (1) "Notwithstanding anything in this Constitution" / "इस संविधान में किसी बात के होते हुए भी"; (2) "Subject to the provisions of this Constitution" / "इस संविधान के उपबंधों के अधीन"; (3) "By law made by Parliament" / "संसद द्वारा बनाई गई विधि द्वारा"; (4) "By order made by the President" / "राष्ट्रपति द्वारा किए गए आदेश द्वारा"; (5) "Laid before each House of Parliament" / "संसद के प्रत्येक सदन के समक्ष रखा जाए"; (6) "Special majority of total membership and two-thirds of members present and voting" / "कुल सदस्य संख्या का बहुमत और उपस्थित तथा मत देने वाले सदस्यों के दो-तिहाई बहुमत"; (7) "Subjective satisfaction" / "व्यक्तिपरक समाधान"; (8) "Mala fide" / "दुर्भावना"; (9) "Repugnant to" / "प्रतिकूल"; (10) "Occupying the field" / "क्षेत्र को पूर्णतः आच्छादित करना"; (11) "Reserved for the consideration of the President" / "राष्ट्रपति के विचारार्थ आरक्षित"; (12) "Received the assent of the President" / "राष्ट्रपति की अनुमति प्राप्त". Write these on your rough sheet in the first five minutes; they appear in 60% of constitutional translation items.

Translation of Anti-Defection Law Proceedings: Tenth Schedule, Paragraph 2–6, and Presiding Officer Decisions

The Tenth Schedule (inserted by the 52nd Amendment, 1985; amended by the 91st Amendment, 2003) generates a distinct genre: disqualification petitions, Presiding Officer’s orders, and judicial review writ petitions. The core operative provision, Paragraph 2(1), disqualifies a member who “has voluntarily given up his membership of such political party” or “votes or abstains from voting in such House contrary to any direction issued by the political party”. The phrase “voluntarily given up his membership” (स्वेच्छा से अपनी सदस्यता त्याग दी है) has been judicially interpreted in Ravi S. Naik v. Union of India (1994) and G. Viswanathan v. Speaker, Tamil Nadu Legislative Assembly (1996) to include conduct evidencing resignation without formal letter; the translator must not narrow “voluntarily given up” to formal resignation only. Paragraph 2(1)(b) — “votes or abstains from voting … contrary to any direction issued by the political party” — requires precise rendering of “whip” (व्हिप / पार्टी निर्देश) and “abstains from voting” (मतदान से विरत रहता है). The exception in Paragraph 4 (merger) applies when “not less than two-thirds of the members of the legislature party concerned have agreed to such merger”. The threshold “not less than two-thirds” (दो-तिहाई से कम नहीं) is arithmetic; any translation rendering it as “more than two-thirds” (दो-तिहाई से अधिक) changes the legal standard. Paragraph 6 vests the final decision on disqualification in the Chairman or Speaker, “and his decision shall be final”. The word “final” (अंतिम) was read down in Kihoto Hollohan v. Zachillhu (1992) to permit judicial review on grounds of mala fide, perversity, violation of natural justice; translations of Kihoto Hollohan reasoning must preserve “jurisdictional error”, “non-application of mind”, “colourable exercise of power”. The Presiding Officer’s order typically follows a structure: (i) recital of petition and parties, (ii) summary of allegations, (iii) evidence considered (affidavits, whip records, video footage), (iv) findings on each ground, (v) operative order of disqualification or dismissal. The phrase “on the basis of the material on record” (अभिलेख पर उपलब्ध सामग्री के आधार पर) and “for reasons to be recorded in writing” (लिखित रूप में दर्ज किए जाने वाले कारणों के लिए) are mandatory. The 91st Amendment deleted Paragraph 3 (split exception) and added a Paragraph 2(1)(b) proviso that a member shall not be disqualified if his vote/abstention is condoned by the party within 15 days; the 15-day condonation window (“पंद्रह दिन की अवधि के भीतर”) must be rendered exactly. When translating the Presiding Officer’s order, the honorific address “Hon’ble Speaker/Chairman” (माननीय अध्यक्ष/सभापति) and the self-reference “I have considered” (मैंने विचार किया है) or “The undersigned has considered” (अधोहस्ताक्षरी ने विचार किया है) follow institutional convention; shifting to third person (“The Speaker has considered”) is acceptable only if the original uses it.

Translation of Finance Commission Reports and Fiscal Federalism Instruments: Terms of Reference, Devolution Formula, and Grants-in-Aid

Finance Commission reports (constituted under Article 280 every five years) and the accompanying Terms of Reference (ToR) notifications, Presidential orders on devolution, and Action Taken Reports constitute a specialised fiscal translation domain. The ToR notification uses formulaic language: “The President, in pursuance of Article 280(1) of the Constitution, hereby constitutes a Finance Commission consisting of a Chairman and four other members…”. The phrase “in pursuance of” (अनुसरण में / के अनुसरण में) and “hereby constitutes” (इस द्वारा गठित करता है) are fixed. The ToR typically directs the Commission to make recommendations on: (a) the distribution between the Union and the States of the net proceeds of taxes (वितरण), (b) the principles governing grants-in-aid to States (सहायता अनुदान के सिद्धांत), (c) measures to augment the Consolidated Fund of a State to supplement the resources of Panchayats and Municipalities (पंचायतों और नगरपालिकाओं के संसाधनों की पूर्ति के लिए राज्य की संचित निधि को बढ़ाने के उपाय). The term “net proceeds” (शुद्ध आय) is defined in Article 279; the translator must not render it as “total proceeds” (कुल आय). The devolution formula uses technical vocabulary: “vertical devolution” (ऊर्ध्वाधर हस्तांतरण / केंद्रीय-राज्य विभाजन), “horizontal devolution” (क्षैतिज हस्तांतरण / अंतर-राज्य विभाजन), “divisible pool” (विभाज्य पूल), “income distance” (आय दूरी), “population” (जनसंख्या — with Census year specified, e.g., 1971 or 2011), “forest and ecology” (वन और पारिस्थितिकी), “demographic performance” (जनसांख्यिकीय प्रदर्शन), “tax effort” (कर प्रयास). The 15th Finance Commission (2020–25) introduced “demographic performance” as a criterion rewarding States for fertility reduction; the term must not be confused with “population”. Grants-in-aid categories: “revenue deficit grants” (राजस्व घाटा अनुदान), “sector-specific grants” (क्षेत्र-विशिष्ट अनुदान — health, education, agriculture), “performance-based grants” (प्रदर्शन-आधारित अनुदान), “grants for local bodies” (स्थानीय निकायों के लिए अनुदान). The Presidential order accepting recommendations uses the formula: “The President, after considering the recommendations of the Fifteenth Finance Commission, hereby makes the following order…”. The phrase “after considering the recommendations” (सिफारिशों पर विचार करने के उपरांत) and “hereby makes the following order” (निम्नलिखित आदेश करता है) are invariant. The Action Taken Report tabled in Parliament uses “The Government has accepted the following recommendations…” and “The Government has not accepted the following recommendations…” with reasons; the translator must preserve the distinction between “accepted” (स्वीकार किया गया), “accepted with modifications” (संशोधनों के साथ स्वीकार किया गया), and “not accepted” (स्वीकार नहीं किया गया).

Translation of Official Language Implementation Instruments: Section 3(3) of the Official Languages Act, 1963, Rule 5 of the Official Languages Rules, 1976, and Bilingual Notifications

The Official Languages Act, 1963 (as amended 1967) and the Official Languages Rules, 1976 generate a high-volume translation stream: Section 3(3) notifications (general orders, resolutions, rules, notifications, administrative reports, press communiqués, contracts, agreements, licences, permits, tender notices, forms), Rule 5 correspondence (communications between Central Government offices and States/offices in Region A, B, C), and the Annual Programme for implementation. Section 3(3) mandates that “every general order, resolution, rule, notification, administrative or other report, press communiqué, contract, agreement, licence, permit, tender notice and form issued by the Central Government shall be in both Hindi and English”. The list of document types is exhaustive; the translator must use the standard nomenclature: “general order” (सामान्य आदेश), “resolution” (संकल्प), “rule” (नियम), “notification” (अधिसूचना), “administrative report” (प्रशासनिक रिपोर्ट), “press communiqué” (प्रेस विज्ञप्ति), “contract” (संविदा), “agreement” (करार), “licence” (अनुज्ञप्ति), “permit” (अनुमति), “tender notice” (निविदा सूचना), “form” (फॉर्म / प्रपत्र). Rule 5 classifies States into Region A (Hindi-speaking), Region B (non-Hindi-speaking but willing), Region C (others); communications to Region A must be in Hindi, to Region C in English, to Region B in both. The labels “Region A”, “Region B”, “Region C” (क्षेत्र ‘क’, क्षेत्र ‘ख’, क्षेत्र ‘ग’) are retained as designated categories. The Annual Programme issued by the Department of Official Language sets targets: “percentage of correspondence in Hindi”, “percentage of original drafting in Hindi”, “training of employees in Hindi typing/steno”. The term “original drafting in Hindi” (मूल प्रारूपण हिंदी में) distinguishes composition from translation; the translator must not conflate the two. Notifications under Section 3(3) often carry a bilingual header: “Government of India / भारत सरकार”, “Ministry of … / … मंत्रालय”, “Notification / अधिसूचना”, “New Delhi, dated … / नई दिल्ली, दिनांक …”. The date format in Hindi follows the Saka era optionally but the Gregorian date is mandatory; both must appear. The signature block: “By order and in the name of the President / राष्ट्रपति के आदेश से और उनके नाम से”, “(Name) / (नाम)”, “Joint Secretary / संयुक्त सचिव”. The phrase “By order and in the name of the President” is a constitutional formula (Article 77(2)); its Hindi rendering is fixed and must not be varied to “राष्ट्रपति की ओर से” or “राष्ट्रपति के नाम पर”.

  • Rendering “repugnant to” as “विरुद्ध” instead of the legally settled “प्रतिकूल” in Article 254 and Tenth Schedule contexts, which changes the judicial test from direct conflict to mere inconsistency.
  • Translating “not less than two-thirds” (Paragraph 4, Tenth Schedule) as “दो-तिहाई से अधिक” (more than two-thirds), raising the merger threshold and misstating the constitutional arithmetic.
  • Using “आंतरिक अशांति” (internal disturbance) for “armed rebellion” in Article 352 Proclamations, ignoring the 44th Amendment substitution.
  • Rendering “net proceeds” (Article 279) as “कुल आय” (total proceeds) in Finance Commission devolution texts, inflating the divisible pool and distorting the fiscal transfer calculation.
  • Translating “original drafting in Hindi” (मूल प्रारूपण हिंदी में) as “हिंदी में अनुवाद” (translation into Hindi), conflating composition with translation and misrepresenting the Annual Programme target.
  • Omitting the fixed formula “इस संविधान में किसी बात के होते हुए भी” for “Notwithstanding anything in this Constitution” and substituting a paraphrase, which fails to signal the constitutional override effect.
  • Shifting the Presiding Officer’s first-person order (“I have considered”) to third person (“The Speaker considered”) when the source uses first person, altering the authoritative voice of the quasi-judicial decision.

Translation of Parliamentary Committee Reports: Evidence, Dissent, and Recommendation Formulas

Parliamentary committee reports — Public Accounts Committee (PAC), Estimates Committee, Committee on Public Undertakings (COPU), Department-Related Standing Committees (DRSCs) — follow a rigid internal architecture that translators must replicate without flattening. The report opens with a composition statement: "The Committee, having been nominated by the Speaker / Chairman, present their Xth Report on ..." The Hindi rendering uses "समिति, जिसे अध्यक्ष / सभापति द्वारा नामित किया गया है, अपना xवां प्रतिवेदन ... प्रस्तुत करती है". The verb "present" (प्रस्तुत करती है) agrees with the collective noun "Committee" treated as singular in Hindi formal usage, unlike English where "Committee" may take plural "present their".

The evidentiary chain is signalled by fixed formulas: "The Committee took evidence of the following witnesses on ..." → "समिति ने निम्नलिखित साक्षियों का साक्ष्य दिनांक ... को लिया". "The Committee also received written memoranda from ..." → "समिति को ... से लिखित ज्ञापन भी प्राप्त हुए". The distinction between oral evidence (मौखिक साक्ष्य) and written memoranda (लिखित ज्ञापन) must be preserved because the rules of procedure treat them differently for confidentiality and publication.

Findings paragraphs use a graded vocabulary: "The Committee observe that ..." (समिति का मत है कि ...), "The Committee note with concern that ..." (समिति चिंता के साथ टिप्पणी करती है कि ...), "The Committee are constrained to observe that ..." (समिति यह टिप्पणी करने के लिए विवश है कि ...), "The Committee strongly deprecate ..." (समिति कड़े शब्दों में निंदा करती है ...). Each escalation maps to a specific Hindi verb: "मत है" (opinion), "टिप्पणी करती है" (observation), "विवश है" (compelled), "निंदा करती है" (deprecation). Substituting "विचार है" for "मत है" or "खेद प्रकट करती है" for "निंदा करती है" dilutes the parliamentary tone.

Dissent notes (असहमति टिप्पणी) and minutes of dissent (असहमति विवरण) are distinct. A dissent note is appended by a member who disagrees with conclusions but not the report's adoption; a minute of dissent records fundamental disagreement and may recommend non-adoption. The header "Minute of Dissent by Shri ..." translates as "श्री ... का असहमति विवरण". The phrase "I record my minute of dissent" → "मैं अपना असहमति विवरण दर्ज करता हूँ". The verb "record" (दर्ज करता हूँ) is mandatory; "submit" (प्रस्तुत करता हूँ) is incorrect because the minute becomes part of the report ab initio.

Recommendations follow a numbered template: "The Committee recommend that the Ministry/Department should ..." → "समिति सिफारिश करती है कि मंत्रालय/विभाग को ... चाहिए". The modal "should" (चाहिए) carries obligatory force in committee parlance; "may" (कर सकता है) is reserved for suggestions. "The Committee desire that ..." (समिति की इच्छा है कि ...) signals a stronger expectation than "recommend" and is used when the executive has discretion but parliamentary will is clear. "The Committee urge that ..." (समिति आग्रह करती है कि ...) appears in time-bound action items. The translator must not collapse these into a single "सिफारिश करती है".

Action Taken Reports (ATRs) tabled pursuant to Direction 73A (Lok Sabha) / Rule 269 (Rajya Sabha) use a binary classification: "Accepted" (स्वीकार किया गया), "Accepted with modifications" (संशोधनों के साथ स्वीकार किया गया), "Not accepted" (स्वीकार नहीं किया गया). The phrase "The Government have not accepted the recommendation for the reasons that ..." → "सरकार ने निम्नलिखित कारणों से सिफारिश स्वीकार नहीं की है ...". The word "reasons" (कारण) is plural even for a single reason; the Hindi plural "कारणों" is obligatory.

Translation of Ordinances, Promulgation Texts, and Re-promulgation Constitutional Constraints

An Ordinance under Article 123 (President) or Article 213 (Governor) carries a promulgation formula that is constitutionally prescribed and judicially scrutinised. The English text opens: "In exercise of the powers conferred by clause (1) of Article 123 of the Constitution, the President is pleased to promulgate the following Ordinance:" The Hindi rendering is fixed: "संविधान के अनुच्छेद 123 के खंड (1) द्वारा प्रदत्त शक्तियों का प्रयोग करते हुए, राष्ट्रपति निम्नलिखित अध्यादेश प्रख्यापित करते हैं:" The verb "promulgate" maps to "प्रख्यापित करते हैं" (plural honorific), not "जारी करते हैं" (issue) or "घोषित करते हैं" (declare). "Pleased to" (प्रसन्नतापूर्वक) is omitted in Hindi because the honorific plural "करते हैं" encodes the constitutional act; inserting "प्रसन्नतापूर्वक" creates a stylistic redundancy unknown in the authentic Hindi text.

The preamble recites necessity: "Whereas Parliament is not in session and the President is satisfied that circumstances exist which render it necessary for him to take immediate action..." → "यथास्थिति, संसद सत्र में नहीं है और राष्ट्रपति का समाधान हो गया है कि ऐसी परिस्थितियाँ विद्यमान हैं जो उनके लिए तत्काल कार्रवाई करना आवश्यक बनाती हैं..." The phrase "is satisfied that" (का समाधान हो गया है कि) is a constitutional standard of subjective satisfaction; translating as "संतुष्ट है कि" (is satisfied) without the perfective "हो गया है" loses the completed mental act required by judicial review (R.C. Cooper v. Union of India, 1970; D.C. Wadhwa v. State of Bihar, 1987).
